The intent is read from the ADDRESS through `subscribeAppLocation`, never from a remembered click, so Back moves the mark, a blocked navigation never moves it, and a deep link arrives already marked; `shared/railOpenIntent.ts` states the rule once — obey the address while it stays inside the rail's own family (including the section-less root a collapsing second press lands on), and otherwise keep wearing the committed tab so the marks do not blink out over the fade the rail is leaving through. A sibling rail derives its family from the hrefs its host hands it, because the Enchiridion's section rail mounts under both `/enchiridion/…` and `/run\|/play/strategikon/enchiridion/…`. Ships on the main menu's mode rail, the Enchiridion's section rail and the Strategikon's; Settings and the Campaign Editor are unchanged and may opt in through the same helper, but no surface may hand-roll the mark. | accepted | 2026-08-10 |
| [0562](0562-the-destination-mounts-at-transition-priority.md) | The scene director commits its destination inside `startTransition`, and nothing in the app measures the DOM during React's render phase. Pressing a rail tab whose destination is expensive used to freeze the app outright — one 1194ms task with no paint in it on the Enchiridion's 284-card gallery, measured live: the rain (a rAF canvas draw) and the waterfalls (`background-position` under `steps()`, never composited) both stopped, and the `›` open mark ([ADR-0561](0561-a-rail-tab-marks-what-it-opened-on-the-press.md)) did not appear until it ended, because the mount ran at SYNCHRONOUS priority (`performSyncWorkOnRoot → renderRootSync → workLoopSync`) and batched the mark's update into the same un-yieldable commit. Two forced synchronous layouts sat inside that commit and made it worse: `KitScroll` read `el.clientHeight` inside a `setState` updater, which React runs during the render phase and re-runs on every render before the state settles, and `FittedTabLabel` read `document.fonts.ready` per label, a getter that settles the font set and so forces a document-wide style recalc. Neither was individually the cause — stubbing either changed nothing, because the next forced layout in the same commit paid the same bill — which is the general lesson: an atomic commit is the defect, not whichever call happens to flush it. Now the three dispatches that swap the mounted scene run in a transition (with `setPath`/`setSearch` inside it, since they are the address that scene renders from), the scroll measurement is hoisted into the effect that already measured, and the font set is read once at module scope. The mark paints ~80ms after the press instead of at the end of the block, an ordinary press's 110ms freeze became 53ms, and the gallery's single task became 735ms + 466ms with React yielding between. A ~1s stretch on that gallery survives and priority cannot remove it: a commit and its layout effects are atomic, and a scroll container must be laid out to be measured — the remaining lever is windowing the catalog, not scheduling. Director behaviour is unchanged and re-gated by `verify:play-transition`, `verify:strategikon`, `verify:scene-retention`, `verify:unit-arrival --settled` and `verify:run-scenes`. | accepted | 2026-08-10 |
| [0563](0563-a-long-list-arrives-in-pieces.md) | A screen that mounts a long list puts it up in PIECES, one batch per animation frame (`useProgressiveMount`), and marks its off-screen items `content-visibility: auto`. The Enchiridion's 284-card catalog mounted in one commit and blocked the app for about a second — the rain (a rAF canvas draw) and the waterfalls (`steps()` on `background-position`, never composited) stopped dead, and so did every control. Neither scheduling nor skipping work fixes that on its own: a transition yields BETWEEN commits but never inside one ([ADR-0562](0562-the-destination-mounts-at-transition-priority.md)), and `content-visibility` skips layout and paint for what is off screen but not the cost of creating it. The list does not have to be shorter and the work does not have to be faster — it has to arrive with a paint between the pieces, which is the whole of the owner's requirement: loading may take as long as it likes, it may not block. Measured on the live app, the press went from one 1194ms task with no paint in it to a run of 50–85ms tasks with the main thread idle 2189ms of the 4s window; the longest stretch without a paint fell from 1074ms to 92ms, and painted frames rose from 154 to 232. The catalog still lands complete (284 in 11 tier groups) and a filter change restarts the fill, which is what `resetKey` is for — a new list of the same length would otherwise arrive in one commit again. An addressed card deeper in the catalog scrolls into view when its batch arrives rather than being missed. | accepted | 2026-08-10 |
| [0561](0561-a-pawn-that-mates-by-arriving-is-paid-and-the-underpromotion-more.md) | A Pawn that promotes into checkmate is paid, and the underpromotion more: `promotion-mate` at **50**, joining en passant and smothered mate at ADR-0540's top pole, and `underpromotion-mate` scaled by the piece chosen — **60 Rook, 80 Bishop or Knight**. These are two of the five ADR-0540 held back *"to see these six in play first, not because they do not fit"*, and both clear its filters without argument: a promotion is defined by an authored square rather than an 8x8 rank, and it announces itself as a `promoted` event on a board whose mate is already settled, so nothing is guessed. They are live content and not an aspiration — `war/store.ts` injects a `run-player-promotion` zone with no `choices` restriction into EVERY Run battle, so all four pieces are offered in every Battle a Run plays. The ladder records the chess rather than a feel: a Rook or a Bishop can never mate where a Queen would not, since on that same square she attacks every square they do, so choosing one is style and costs nothing once the mate is there (the Battle ends either way) — which is why the Rook sits just above the ordinary promotion mate. The Bishop is dearer because a Bishop mate needs a far narrower shape: one colour of diagonal with every other flight answered by other men. **Only the Knight can mate where the Queen cannot**, the Knight's move being the one thing she lacks, and it is paid ALONGSIDE the Bishop rather than above it because what the two ask of a position is nothing alike and ranking them would invent a difference rather than record one. An underpromotion mate pays INSTEAD of the promotion mate on ADR-0540's rungs-of-one-ladder rule (every underpromotion mate *is* a promotion mate), while everything else still stacks — a Knight promoting into a smothered mate earns both. The promoted unit must be AMONG the checkers: a Pawn that queens while another unit mates has not mated by promoting, and that check is already paid as the discovered one it is; *among* rather than *only* keeps a promotion that mates as half a double check. Mate is read, never searched — `kingCheckers` plus `sideHasLegalMove`, the same "no legal action" expression the canonical adjudicator uses (ADR-0059), computed ONCE and shared with the check shapes — so this cannot call a position mate the Battle does not. `priceNote` is BUILT from `RUN_UNDERPROMOTION_MATE_TENTHS` rather than typed beside it, so the Enchiridion sentence cannot drift from the gold paid. The Enchiridion gains both entries on real Battle-renderer boards, the underpromotion drawn as the Knight case on purpose (a QUEEN on that promotion square would not even be giving check, so the picture is the reason the entry exists), and its "How they add up" note corrects two numbers stale since ADR-0547 made the stored tenth the gold — it read `3` and `2` for the two checks while the cards beside it showed 30 and 20. Accepted tension: Deditio (ADR-0543) pays for the force still standing and a promotion mate arrives late, so the slow win that walks a Pawn home banks a smaller Deditio — a genuine choice between two ways to end a Battle, the first the Run has offered. `verify:manubiae` covers `promotion-mate` and CANNOT cover `underpromotion-mate`, since it plans moves with the board's default promotion (the Queen) and the choice happens in the picker after the move is authored; the payment path is shared. Board law untouched; no RunSaveVersion, save shape, or database migration change | accepted; refines [0540](0540-the-board-pays-manubiae-for-named-tactics.md)/[0543](0543-a-mate-is-paid-for-the-army-it-did-not-have-to-take.md) | 2026-08-10 |
| [0562](0562-the-mate-pays-once-and-pays-more-the-less-gives-it.md) | `humble-mate` pays for the distance the mating unit falls short of a Queen, at **three gold a point** — Rook 12, Bishop or Knight 18, Pawn 24 — and because a Queen is 9, **her mate comes out at exactly nothing**, so "anything but a Queen" is what the arithmetic SAYS rather than a clause bolted onto it (the shape `RUN_ROYAL_FORK_MIN_VICTIM_VALUE` already has). Three a point rather than the two an advantageous capture and Deditio pay, since this is counted once per Battle at most while those are counted many times, but still at ADR-0540's ten-gold pole — *"the Run noticing something you were going to do anyway"* — because a Rook mate is simply how a Battle usually ends; the GRADIENT is the teaching, doubling from Rook to Pawn being what makes it worth looking for the Knight's mate instead of the Queen's. The load-bearing decision is **THE MATE PAYS ONCE**: every Battle is won by checkmate (ADR-0543), and FOUR entries now describe one — what the mating unit is worth, what the King's own men were doing around it, and whether that unit arrived by promoting — so left to stack, a Knight underpromotion into a smothered mate would collect all four for one mate. They are rungs of one ladder in exactly the sense ADR-0540 gave the two checks, since a smothered mate IS a mate by a Knight and an underpromotion mate IS a mate by the lesser piece the Pawn chose; the candidates are gathered and the DEAREST is paid, `smotheredMateBy` moving out of the per-moved-piece loop into that group so one decision is made rather than a push racing the others. Every rung above the floor is dearer than the whole of the floor's range (50/50/60/80 against a top of 24), so "the dearest pays" and "the most specific pays" are the SAME rule here rather than two that can disagree — deliberate, and a constraint on any future rung. Everything outside the mate still stacks. Paid on the LEAST valuable checker, since when two units mate at once the deed is the smaller of them. It reads the piece's CURRENT type where every other Manubium reads `promotedFrom`, and the divergence is the point: what this entry rewards is a CREATIVE mate rather than the Queen mate that is the default ending of every game of chess, a Queen delivering mate is not special, and it does not become special because the Queen used to be a Pawn — the board at that moment holds a Queen doing the most ordinary thing a Queen does. `manubiaeUnitWorth` answers a genuinely different question, *what did this cost the Run*, which is why ADR-0540 rightly prices a queened Pawn as a Pawn on both sides of a CAPTURE; both readers are correct for their own question and neither generalizes to the other, so `boardPieceValue` is a separate reader with its own name rather than a flag on the existing one. The clause was reversed once and reverted, so the ADR records the tempting argument and why it fails: trading down to a Pawn advantage and walking a passed Pawn home IS a lean skilful win and pricing that Queen at nine does pay nothing for the endgame — all true, and still not what this entry is for, because this pays for the SHAPE OF THE MATE and a grind-out finishing with a Queen has no unusual shape however it was reached. A grind-out is a player choice and it stays UNREWARDED on purpose, which is not an oversight for a later entry to close: a grind-out is not desirable and it means the level was not good enough to force interesting decisions, so paying for it would pay for the level's failure and point players at the levels that fail. The remedy for a Battle that grinds is the BATTLE — material, terrain, objective, clock — not a position-shaped bounty ("won with little on the board", "won an endgame", "won from behind"); ADR-0543's Deditio already prices the symptom, which is why it is priced DOWN rather than merely priced differently. The King is refused explicitly, its zero being a sentinel for "never bought" that would otherwise read as the humblest unit and pay the most; it cannot arise, since a King may not give check, but the scale is not asked to be lucky about it. The Enchiridion entry is drawn as a PAWN's mate — four of the King's own men sealing it in, the fifth square held by the mating Pawn, a second Pawn defending it — and the "How they add up" note gains the pays-once rule. Accepted consequences: this is the first Manubium collected as a matter of course rather than as a flourish, so the Run's floor rises on nearly every Battle (12 on the ordinary Rook mate against a Battle reward near 80), and the pays-once rule is what stops it compounding. It points the SAME way as Deditio (ADR-0543) rather than against it: this ladder's FLOOR, a Rook's 12, is reachable at the end of a long endgame, but its TOP is not — the Knight, Bishop and Pawn mates at 18 and 24 need the enemy King boxed in by its own men or caught in a sharp position, which happens with force still on the board — so the reward is concentrated where Deditio's is and a grind-out collects the least from both. A player who queens a Pawn now has a reason not to mate with her, which is the game teaching that the last move is a choice and not a formality. Board law untouched; no RunSaveVersion, save shape, or database migration change | accepted; refines [0540](0540-the-board-pays-manubiae-for-named-tactics.md)/[0543](0543-a-mate-is-paid-for-the-army-it-did-not-have-to-take.md)/[0561](0561-a-pawn-that-mates-by-arriving-is-paid-and-the-underpromotion-more.md) | 2026-08-10 |
| [0564](0564-eight-squares-of-reach-is-a-deed.md) | Two reach entries — `long-capture` at **10** and `long-check` at **20** — for a deed that spans **eight squares**, the width of a standard chessboard, which no board in this game is, and that is the point: it is the one reach every player already has a feel for. This is the first Manubium to price DISTANCE rather than a relationship between pieces, which ADR-0540 deliberately chose (motifs "defined by *local relationships between pieces*, which read the same on any board"); it survives that ADR's two filters anyway, since a distance is read off two squares of a committed board with no search and no opponent reply. The capture pays the ten-gold "the Run noticing something you were going to do anyway" pole because it is usually a capture the player wanted anyway with the distance as flourish; the check pays double because it had to be ENGINEERED — the enemy King has to be on that line, eight away, with nothing between. Counted along the LINE and not as both axes added up: `max(|dx|,|dy|)`, so a Rook's eight along a rank and a Bishop's eight along a diagonal are the same eight, where summing would make the Bishop's eight into sixteen and one threshold would mean a different thing to every unit — the discriminating case is pinned in tests, a SEVEN-square diagonal (fourteen by the wrong metric) paying nothing. A capture is measured on the MOVE (set-out square to landing square) and a check on the LINE THE CHECK RUNS (checker to King), two different questions each taking its own answer, which means the checker NEED NOT be the unit that moved — a line you opened reaches from wherever the unit behind it stands — so a discovery from across the board earns the discovered check AND the long check. Paid once per check on the furthest-reaching checker, each checker paired with the King it is actually attacking through the same `attacksSquare` geometry check detection uses, so a board fielding more than one enemy King cannot be credited with a line it does not have. Any unit may earn it and the type is never consulted: only line pieces reach eight in practice, but nothing says so, because that is the board's business and not a rule. Everything STACKS — reach is orthogonal to material and to the shape of a check, so a Rook crossing the board to take a Queen earns both, and nothing here is a kind of anything else. A board smaller than nine in both dimensions can never offer these and no arrangement is made for it. The two Enchiridion diagrams break the section's five-square convention at 9x5 and 9x9 because the deed does not fit on a smaller board, each marking the whole lane crossed so the distance is the picture rather than a number in the copy. Consequences: this is the first Manubium whose availability is a property of the LEVEL rather than the play — the War's Battles run from three squares wide to nine, so some cannot offer it at all — and that unevenness is not a defect but a lever level authoring did not have, since a big open board is now one where long-range play pays. Terrain is what makes it rare (water halts, walls and fences close crossings, every unit in the way blocks), so it rewards recognizing open ground, a thing this game has and chess does not. A Knight can never earn either, the first entry with a unit structurally shut out. Board law untouched; no RunSaveVersion, save shape, or database migration change | accepted; refines [0540](0540-the-board-pays-manubiae-for-named-tactics.md) | 2026-08-10 |
| [0565](0565-a-knights-fork-is-paid-by-the-prong-and-accelerates.md) | `knight-fork` pays by the PRONG and the price ACCELERATES: the second unit attacked adds one rung, the third adds two, the fourth adds three, giving **5, 15, 30, 50, 75** for two through six units. ADR-0527's royal fork asks about the QUALITY of two prongs (the King, and a Rook or better) and says nothing about how many things a unit hits; this is the other question. Knight-specific, and the geometry is why: a Knight's attacks cannot be blocked and its eight squares are SCATTERED rather than laid along lines, so the enemy has no move answering two at once — no interposition, no single retreat covering both, no counter-line — where every other piece forks along rays a block or a pin can address. A flat rate per unit would say a Knight hitting four things is twice a Knight hitting two and it is nothing of the sort: the second prong is what makes it a fork, and every prong after that is another square the enemy cannot answer. Five a rung rather than ten so the plain two-prong fork lands UNDER the royal fork it is often a lesser version of, with three prongs passing it — that order is the whole reason for the number. COUNT, not value: a Knight forking three Pawns pays 15 and that is correct, and the rate is low enough that counting bodies never outruns quality by much (the royal fork's King-and-Queen at 10 sits between the two-prong 5 and the three-prong 15). The two forks are ONE LADDER — a Knight striking the King and a Rook is both, one unit's fork is one deed, so the dearer pays and the other stands down, the rule the two checks and the four mates already run on — which also means `forkHolds` is asked once, of the FORK rather than of an entry, and only when there is a fork to ask about. The fork must HOLD and it matters MORE here than for the royal one: a royal fork is a CHECK the enemy must answer, while a fork of three ordinary pieces forces nothing at all, so a Knight planted en prise beside three enemies has simply handed over a Knight and paying for it would teach exactly that move. Membership is `isEnemy`, the predicate a capture uses, so obstacles and neutrals are not attacked things; `enemiesAttackedBy` joins `royalForkVictim` in `core/rules` as its counting counterpart through the same `attacksSquare` geometry, deliberately NOT `opponentsUnderAttackBy`, which serves the service record and counts neutrals. From the square it just moved to, as the royal fork requires. Consequence worth noticing: the Knight now has a coherent identity in this economy and it is the identity chess gives it — the only unit that can earn a smothered mate (ADR-0540), the only one whose underpromotion can mate where a Queen could not (ADR-0561), now the only one paid for the breadth of what it attacks, and the one unit structurally shut out of the reach bounties since its longest move is two squares (ADR-0564). Paid for what cannot be blocked and cannot be answered; never paid for distance. Nothing coordinated that; it fell out of pricing each deed honestly, and is worth checking before adding an entry that breaks it. Board law untouched; no RunSaveVersion, save shape, or database migration change | accepted; refines [0527](0527-a-royal-fork-pays-one-gold.md)/[0540](0540-the-board-pays-manubiae-for-named-tactics.md); prong membership refined by [0566](0566-a-knights-fork-counts-prongs-the-enemy-cannot-answer.md) | 2026-08-10 |
| [0566](0566-a-knights-fork-counts-prongs-the-enemy-cannot-answer.md) | A `knight-fork` prong is something the enemy CANNOT ANSWER, and there are exactly two kinds: their King, because it must move, and an UNDEFENDED unit, because the enemy gets one move and it cannot save two of them. The two kinds are INTERCHANGEABLE — the King and one free piece, or two free pieces, are the same fork of two and pay the same 5; neither kind is required and neither is worth more, because what is paid for is the enemy's inability to answer rather than the presence of a check. ADR-0565 counted every unit struck, and the count is not the deed: a Knight landing among a chain of mutually defended Pawns strikes three units and wins nothing, since they leave every one where it stands and the Knight that takes one is taken straight back. So a DEFENDED unit is no prong and is not counted, and the King is EXEMPT from that question rather than special-cased into it — a King cannot be taken at all, so "is it defended" does not apply, which is one clause in the filter rather than a second branch. This is where the two forks part company, deliberately: ADR-0527's royal fork still never asks whether its victim is defended, because a Rook won for a Knight is worth the exchange even when they take back — QUALITY survives a recapture, COUNT does not, and the test pinning them apart is the defended Rook beside the King (no Knight's fork, still a royal one). ADR-0565's ladder is untouched and now reads as prongs rather than bodies: two at 5, three at 15, four at 30, still under and then over the royal fork's 10. "Undefended" is the plain board reading — no other living unit of that side attacks the square it stands on, their King counting as a defender — and it is attack geometry, not legality: a defender pinned against its own King still counts, because the question is what guards the square rather than what the position would survive. `unitIsDefended` joins `enemiesAttackedBy`/`royalForkVictim` in `core/rules` through the same `attacksSquare` geometry; `forkHolds` is unchanged and still asked of the fork, the two being halves of one question (can the enemy profitably take the KNIGHT; can the Knight profitably take a PRONG). Consequence: the bounty gets harder to earn by accident without getting rare — a Knight landing where two enemy pieces happen to stand no longer pays, where two HANGING pieces still does, so what was removed is exactly the case that wins nothing. Catalog sentence and Enchiridion diagram rewritten to the new shape (one prong of each kind), because an example that does not earn the deed teaches the wrong rule harder than no example would. Board law, AI, RunSaveVersion, save shape and database migrations untouched; gold already earned under the old reading is kept | accepted; refines [0565](0565-a-knights-fork-is-paid-by-the-prong-and-accelerates.md)/[0540](0540-the-board-pays-manubiae-for-named-tactics.md) | 2026-08-10 |
| [0567](0567-rarity-is-a-flat-cut-on-price-and-a-list-of-declared-shifts.md) | Rarity becomes a flat cut on the printed price (Common through 70 gold, Uncommon through 100, Rare above, priced on the canonical density curve so a card's tier is a catalog property) followed by an ordered list of DECLARED SHIFTS — a shift is data carrying an id, a name, its reason and a predicate, naming a `from` tier to MOVE cards out of it or omitting one to INTRODUCE them from wherever they sit — and `runRarityShiftAudit` reports per shift how many cards it actually moved so a dead shift reads as dead in the Card Pool studio instead of surfacing weeks later as a Sectio dealing the same card twice; one shift ships, taking every card of nothing but minors into Rare because free placement makes two Bishops the pair and three minors an assembled battery that price cannot see; ADR-0523's awkward-footprint demotion retires because a density price already discounts a shape that packs badly, and ADR-0532's Bishop step retires into the cluster shift, which reaches the Knight pair on the same argument; the shipped market goes 6/9/54 to 41/14/14, duplicate Sectio rows fall from 22.6% to 0.31%, the opening ceiling now empties Uncommon rather than Rare (19/0/1), and rarity stays derived so no RunSaveVersion bump | accepted; supersedes ADR-0523's material band and ADR-0532's Bishop step | 2026-08-10 |
| [0568](0568-the-sectio-hands-the-player-back-to-its-victory.md) | A won Battle's report OUTLIVES its own screen: `run.aftermath` is retained through `aftermath`, the Conflict's `bona-vacantia` and the `sectio` its gold is spent in, and the Sectio carries **Back to Victory** beside Continue — because the moment a player is pricing a formation card is exactly the moment they ask what the fight paid, and Continue had already thrown the answer away. It is a REVIEW and never a rewind, for a sharper version of the reason ADR-0455 gave when the report gained its own Back to the board: leaving the report banks the reward, draws the Sectio's offers off a hidden seed-derived pile and may reveal the After-Hours Key's lipsanon, and by then the player may have admitted one card and struck another — so a rewind would un-bank gold that is partly spent and put back cards nobody asked to un-buy. Instead the Sectio STANDS behind the report being read, and `leaveAftermath` on a report whose Sectio already exists RETURNS to it rather than re-running the transition, so the reward banks once, the pile is drawn from once, and the round trip may be made any number of times. A retained report may only describe the Battle its screen followed; one naming another is dropped by the normalizer and refused by the server. The terminal-board snapshot is retired one screen later than ADR-0455 put it — at the Sectio's Continue rather than the report's — so the report reached back to still presents its own Back to the exact won board. The server's phase-shaped invariants gain exactly one paired exception, checked by identity: a `sectio` may stand in phase `aftermath`, and that reviewed report alone may carry no `battleRuntime`, but only when the two name the same Battle. The crafter closes each fast-forwarded Battle and leaves its report rather than passing it, so a `craft=sectio` link can be pressed straight through to the Victory screen; accounting is untouched (nothing taken, so nothing surrendered, and the clock reads the instant it already read) and only the turn count is dressed, which pays nothing under ADR-0539. No RunSaveVersion, migration, reward formula or Battle rule change: an older document simply carries no report and says so with an unpressable control rather than by dropping one out of the rail | accepted; refines [0377](0377-a-won-battle-is-reported-on-its-own-screen.md)/[0452](0452-a-won-run-battle-pauses-on-its-visible-board-before-rewards.md)/[0455](0455-aftermath-retains-a-reversible-terminal-board-review.md) | 2026-08-10 |
| [0569](0569-the-invariant-title-bar-cluster-is-one-divided-box.md) | The invariant trailing title-bar cluster — music, the Settings gear, the account control — is ONE `DividedInnerChromeBox` rather than three separately framed `inner-box` units in a flex row. Between two glyphs the bar used to show three edges (a frame rail, a strip of itself, another frame rail) to say one thing; it now shows the box's own single rail, capped where it meets the frame by a junction the topology places ([ADR-0242](0242-divided-inner-grids-own-one-rail-topology.md)). The box's COLUMNS are its members, derived from the seat list, so a bar without the gear declares two columns and one rail rather than leaving an empty compartment. A seat is deliberately NOT a registered chrome unit — the unit is what brings a frame and the box already drew one — so `TitleBarButtonPrimitive` gains `seated`, rendering a plain button on `.titlebar-control--seat`; the leaf oak and its phase stay, because a trigger wears the oak wherever it sits ([ADR-0433](0433-leaf-chrome-uses-oak-over-structural-teal-fields.md)). A compartment is exactly the OPENING the framed button used to give its glyph (`--titlebar-control-seat` = size − two inner rails = 38px), so the same icon meets the same square and the box's outer 52px derives rather than being stated. A TRACK is that opening plus what the rails take back: a rail is drawn ON the grid line and straddles it, so it covers half its width from the cell either side, and equal tracks give UNEQUAL compartments — shipped that way first and the openings measured 34.5 / 31 / 34.5 against a 38 height, none of them square and the gear the odd one out, while every cell rect read a tidy 38. Each track adds `--titlebar-seat-rail-half` per internal side and each seat gives the same half-rail back as padding so its glyph centres in what can be seen; all three openings now measure 38×38 and the cluster comes out 142px against 172px. `.cluster-bgm-slot` stops being `display: contents` and becomes a real cell, since a declared column must exist before `bgm.js` re-homes its button into it or every later member sits one column left until it lands. Route contributions stay individually framed before the persistent divider — that divider is what separates what a screen adds from what the app always carries ([ADR-0104](0104-title-bar-controls-are-typed-contributions-to-one-lane.md)) — which the owner chose explicitly over one box for everything or a second box for the contributions. The music control's two axes collapse to one: a compartment has no frame to light, so the icon carries all three states (bright = playing here, half-lit = playing in another tab, flat grey = silent) rather than losing the follower state back into `is-muted`, the distinction [ADR-0044](0044-persistent-mute-control-in-the-trailing-cluster.md) added. The frameless reset is registered in `check-ui-surface-contract.mjs` beside `.section-box-member-verb` and written two classes deep, because the shell's `button.active` base is (0,1,1) and would otherwise repaint a lit seat with its own gradient. `verify:titlebar` now measures the BOX against the lane and the SEATS against the box — each seat's OPENING (its rect minus any rail overlapping it) must be square and identical, because measuring cells is exactly how the unequal compartments shipped — and fails outright if any seat carries a `data-chrome-unit`, the rendered-DOM statement of no frame inside the frame | accepted; refines [0104](0104-title-bar-controls-are-typed-contributions-to-one-lane.md)/[0242](0242-divided-inner-grids-own-one-rail-topology.md)/[0433](0433-leaf-chrome-uses-oak-over-structural-teal-fields.md)/[0044](0044-persistent-mute-control-in-the-trailing-cluster.md) | 2026-08-10 |
| [0570](0570-a-board-anchored-callout-is-placed-in-screen-pixels-and-stays-inside-the-battlefield.md) | A callout anchored to a board square states its offset in SCREEN pixels past the inverse zoom, and is kept inside `.skirmish-field` — the region the board owns up to the Controls divider. The promotion picker was cut in half by that panel, and two faults put it there. Its `position: absolute` was silently taken away by `.inner-chrome-box.has-chrome-surface-fill`, which gives any inner box with a surface fill `position: relative` later in the same file at equal specificity, so `right`/`left`/`top` stopped meaning offsets from the seat and became nudges from a FLOW box 296 **board** units wide — the callout swung further right the more the player zoomed in (measured live: seat at x=860 opened 827→1123 against a panel starting at 973). And the side was chosen from the sign of the seat's board-space `left`, "toward the board's middle", which is not the screen's middle once the camera has moved, so the rule could aim the box at the panel with confidence. Placement now rides the inline `transform` — `scale(1/zoom) translate(dx, dy)` with `transform-origin: top left`, translating from the element's own static position (the zero-size anchor on the promotion square) — so the offsets are exact screen pixels at every zoom AND land identically whether the box computes to static, relative or absolute; `position` is deleted from the callout's rule so the trap cannot be re-armed by a stylesheet the component cannot see. The side is chosen from the room the screen has and flips if the 296px box will not fit; the result is slid back inside the battlefield on both axes, and a region smaller than the callout pins it to the NEAR edge, an overlapping callout being better than a legible one nobody can click. Geometry unchanged (72px clear of the Pawn, rising 156px) — it was always meant to be screen pixels. Measured in a layout effect on every render while open, because the seat travels whenever the camera moves and a pan is not a prop; an unchanged placement re-renders nothing. Board law, promotion rules and the ADR-0559 timing untouched | accepted; refines [0504](0504-promotion-choices-stay-with-the-arrived-pawn.md)/[0559](0559-a-promotion-asks-as-the-move-is-played-not-as-it-lands.md) | 2026-08-10 |
| [0570](0570-a-pad-of-keys-is-one-divided-box.md) | A PAD of keys is one divided box, and each key is a compartment of it — the owner's "two 9 way controls", the Level Editor's 8-way **Facing** compass (shared with Placed Art, Forest sections and three Studio surfaces) and the **Default facing** popover. Each was nine `inner-tool-square` units in a CSS grid with a 4px gap, so between one letter and the next the panel showed a frame rail, a strip of panel and another frame rail, twice over — nine frames and twelve gaps to say one control — and the popover floated its own hand-drawn panel (`background`/`border`/`border-radius`/`box-shadow`) in front of chrome the kit already draws. `ChromeSeatGrid` is the shared object: a `DividedInnerChromeBox` of R×C equal compartments whose seats are declared as DATA in rows, because a caller that could pass markup could author the space between two keys and a hand-placed rail cannot know where it meets the frame (the reason `ChromeVerbRow` takes verbs). A seat is deliberately NOT a registered chrome unit — the unit brings the frame and the box drew one — so it is a plain button on `.chrome-seat`, registered in `check-ui-surface-contract.mjs` beside `.section-box-member-verb`/`.titlebar-control--seat`; the leaf oak still arrives named on the seat and phased by its place in the data ([ADR-0433](0433-leaf-chrome-uses-oak-over-structural-teal-fields.md)). A compartment is exactly the OPENING the framed square gave its glyph — the tool square minus its own two inner rails, 38 − 14 = 24px, declared once as `--chrome-seat-opening` — so the letters are untouched and the compass goes 122px to 100px. The rail-overlap rule is now stated ONCE for BOTH axes: `chromeDividedSeatAxis` returns the tracks and the seat insets together, where [ADR-0569](0569-the-invariant-title-bar-cluster-is-one-divided-box.md) derived them separately (tracks in TSX, insets in CSS) and shipped 34.5 / 31 / 34.5 compartments against a 38 height; `HeaderAccountCluster` asks the same function, so a rule already got wrong once has one implementation. All nine openings measure 24×24 on a 100×100 box, in the editor and in the Studio. The menu's hollow centre stays a REAL cell showing the box's field, since dropping it would move the three southern directions one column left. State is carried by the GLYPH because a compartment has no frame to light — nothing is lost, as the lit background `.unit-facing-cell.is-active` declared had been painted over by the installed leaf surface for as long as both existed. The popover keeps a bare positioned wrapper: a divided grid positions itself `relative` for its own rail layer and the registry's inner-box rule restates that from a runtime stylesheet at a specificity a class on the box does not reliably beat. Six raw-paint entries leave the surface-debt baseline and none arrive; the seat's focus ring is inset so it stops outlining its neighbours' edges. Shipping it also exposed a real bug in the primitive: a FILLED divided box was painting its rows over its own VERTICAL rails, because `.inner-chrome-box.has-chrome-surface-fill > :not(.inner-chrome-box-fill)` out-specifies the grid and lifted both layers to `z-index: 1` — the rail layer lost its 2 and tied with the rows, which won on DOM order, and the rows layer gained a stacking context that traps the row boundaries' junction atoms (z-index 3) the grid's own rule says must stay outside it. Both are exempted now beside the `position: absolute` exemption already there. No consumer had hit it because none combined a fill, more than one column, and no spanning row: `SectionBox` declares one column, `LevelPreviewColumn` spans its name row into per-row segments, and the title-bar cluster takes no fill. Caught by sampling the scanline off the live capture, after the pad was handed over with the rails invisible and read as present. No gameplay, RunSaveVersion, save shape or database migration change | accepted; refines [0242](0242-divided-inner-grids-own-one-rail-topology.md)/[0569](0569-the-invariant-title-bar-cluster-is-one-divided-box.md)/[0433](0433-leaf-chrome-uses-oak-over-structural-teal-fields.md) | 2026-08-11 |
| [0571](0571-start-new-run-is-one-box-of-cells.md) | Start New Run's detail column is **ONE divided box** and everything in it is a CELL of that box. It was four separate boxes stacked with the live vista showing through the gaps, which drew one control as four unrelated things that happened to be stacked; preparing a Run is a single act, and a gap is what this kit puts between things that are NOT related. Every separation is now a rail the box lays and caps from its own grid lines ([ADR-0242](0242-divided-inner-grids-own-one-rail-topology.md)). The load-bearing half is what a cell IS: **a cell that is a CONTROL takes no inset at all — the control fills the whole area between the rails and the wood reaches them**, which is what INSERTED means here. A registered control seated inside a cell brings its own 9-slice frame, drawing a second rail a few pixels inside the box's own and leaving its wood as a plaque floating on a strip of marble belonging to nothing: a box in a box. So Start Run IS the row (`ChromeDividedGridRow as="button"` wearing `section-box-member-verb`, the frameless reset the kit already owns for a verb whose edge is its box's frame), and every picker is **seated** — `HouseSelect seated`, which names no registered unit because `inner-dropdown` IS the frame, wears the oak across the cell and takes the shared stepper chevron rather than the frame's own. The plate is **30px, not 38**: a framed button's 9-slice eats a rail's width a side so about 24px of it is oak, and a cell painting the full 38 reads half again as heavy as every framed control beside it. A cell that CARRIES something — a name, a sentence — takes `--ds-inset` and wears the marble, which is why the replacement warning being bare marble is now what tells you it cannot be pressed ([ADR-0433](0433-leaf-chrome-uses-oak-over-structural-teal-fields.md)). Options opens from a **square key** (the registered `inner-tool-square`, carrying the shared chevron) seated where the chevron used to hang, since a cell has no frame to be the button and making the cell pressable would put a press on a region whose boundary belongs to something bigger; the key keeps its own frame because it stands ON a marble cell rather than filling one, and it is labelled BY the cell's name, so the screen never says Options twice. There is **one WOOD height through the whole box, measured in oak rather than in boxes**: the key is the only FRAMED control here, and a frame is measured in what it eats — at a plate-sized 30px box its 9-slice rails take 7px a side and leave 16px of oak against the 30px a frameless plate paints, so matching their BOXES made it read half the size of the bars above it. Its box is one plate plus its own two rails (44px), which paints one plate of oak; that is `.ce-preview-box`'s lesson from the other direction, where a frameless cell was shrunk to the oak a framed button paints. Set through `--le-inner-square`, the token the registered unit already reads (scoped to the Level Editor's screen, it never reached the menu shell, where the unit fell back to 38px). **The inset is measured to the OAK** on every side a frame meets — three of the key's four, the fourth being the cell's leading edge where the title's text sits and text has no frame to discount — so the key's wood stops exactly as far from the cell's trailing edge as the title's letters start from its leading one (both 23px from the box's frame, measured live), and **one name-cell seat** comes out the same either way — a 44px key with 9px above and below is the same 62px as a line of text with 16px, which matters because Ataraxia's cell holds only text and left to itself stood shorter than Options, putting the two titles at different heights in what is meant to be the same treatment. The key also CENTRES its mark: `.stepper-glyph` is an inline-block, so a square leaving it to default block layout hangs it on a text baseline, which is what put the chevron 2.5px into the corner; the shared mark is untouched, since captured at 8x and measured off the painted pixels a turned chevron sits 0.19 CSS px off its own glyph's centre — below what the rasterizer can place at 1x, so when a turned chevron looks off-centre the fault is its HOST. What none of this does is line the key up with the BARS above it: their oak reaches the box's own frame because they ARE cells, while the key is a control seated in one and keeps the cell's inset — aligning their wood would mean the key's frame overhanging into the box's, which draws two rails touching. **Its cells are always there, empty when closed** — reserved by keeping the choices LAID OUT and hiding only their paint (`visibility`, never `display: none`), so what is held is exactly what they need and cannot drift from a number in the stylesheet, and a closed section stays out of the tab order and the a11y tree. **The rails go with the paint**: closed, the section is reserved SPACE rather than compartments, so the box's rails stop at the Options name row — a rail divides two things and there is nothing down there to divide yet. The boundary is matched by ADJACENCY (`.chrome-divided-grid__row-boundary:has(+ .run-rules-cell[data-open="false"])`) rather than by a class the row carries, so the grid stays the only thing deciding where a rail IS and the section only says when one is showing. Each rule now states its own NAME beside what the current answer does, and the two group headings are gone: on screen they were three unlabelled pickers under one word, and as separate cells the old shape cost nine compartments and ran the box off the bottom of the column. Measured live, the box is 575px in BOTH states, every control's centre hit-tests to itself, a closed Options cell hit-tests to nothing, and a real click on a seated picker opens its menu — which is what retires [ADR-0475](0475-run-preparation-actions-follow-their-decision-content.md)'s growth argument for seating Options last (the seating survives on the reason that still holds: the defaults are the game, and a section almost nobody opens must not stand between the Ataraxia choice and the verb as if it were a step in setup). Only a DIRECT child of the box is a row it lays a rail around, so Ataraxia and the rule options contribute ARRAYS of cells (`ataraxiaPrepCells`, `useRunRulesCells`) rather than rendering as components, and a fragment would not do, since the box sees one child through it. The armed Keep Run / Abandon and Start pair is the one verb SPLITTING into two compartments of the same row: the box declares its second column only while that question is open, and because every other cell spans, the box suppresses its full-height rail and lets that row carry the one vertical segment itself, capped at both ends by the tees the boundary layer places where it meets the rows above and below. Stacking them was tried first on the mistaken belief that box-wide columns would rule a line down every cell above — they do not, and the split is what the control had before this ADR and what reads as one question with two answers. `SectionBox` is UNCHANGED and still owns the named-group box for Settings groups and the War editor's Battles, where the box still IS the disclosure; this says only that Run preparation is not one of them | accepted; refines [0242](0242-divided-inner-grids-own-one-rail-topology.md)/[0433](0433-leaf-chrome-uses-oak-over-structural-teal-fields.md)/[0475](0475-run-preparation-actions-follow-their-decision-content.md)/[0556](0556-run-preparation-chooses-in-a-tab-column-of-one-line-rows.md) | 2026-08-10 |
| [0572](0572-a-visible-scene-accepts-the-click-that-lands-on-it.md) | Reachability tracks **visibility**, not commit: a scene the player can SEE takes the click that lands on it. `preparing` spans `startup`/`loading`/**`entering`**/`error`, so gating `inert`, `aria-hidden` and `.is-preparing`'s `pointer-events: none` on it held the DOM shut through the entire entrance — the one part of preparation anybody is looking at. Measured cold on the live Level Editor over four runs: the destination carries its final label while unreachable for 1.4–1.9s (harmless, all of it at opacity 0 behind the ladder's curtain), of which **250–320ms is visible** and **up to ~120ms is fully opaque**, because `entrance-finished` comes off the browser's `transitionend` and then waits on a React commit. Maximum opacity reached while unreachable was **1.000** — nothing distinguished the last dead frame from the first live one, so a press on **Publish to all players** produced no confirm dialog, no status-log entry and no request, and the author's only available reading was that the app had silently refused to publish (a synthetic `el.click()` opened the dialog at the same instant, dispatched events ignoring `inert`, which is what identified the gate rather than an overlay). [ADR-0369](0369-one-cold-load-ladder-builds-background-then-chrome-then-scene.md) §5 had already settled the pairing from the other side — `reveal-pending` is "inert as well as invisible", since an `opacity: 0` element still takes clicks — and the entrance was the one place it came apart: inert AND seen. So `SceneBoundary` gates the DOM on `preparing && !revealing`, the host-preserving region lifts on the same signal, and the pointer silence is scoped under `.scene-director:not(.is-entering)` — **withdrawn, never overridden with `pointer-events: auto`**, because an override would blanket-revive decorative layers that declare their own `none` and hand them 350ms of click-stealing per navigation, and because withdrawal preserves the reason each descendant level is named at all (a child opting back into the pointer is not bound by an ancestor's silence). This grants no permission to PERFORM: [ADR-0421](0421-a-preparing-scene-has-no-permission-to-perform.md) is untouched and still owns functional time, motion, entered actions and gameplay mutation, `useSceneActivation()` still becomes true only at commit, and the DOM gate and the activation gate stop being one fact expressed twice. [ADR-0307](0307-every-replaceable-region-is-a-director-owned-scene-slot.md)'s requirement that Battle clocks and input wait for commit is now carried by the screens holding those clocks rather than by a blanket attribute on every route — which is already how the app is built, `Skirmish` gating the board's `interactive` and its clock on activation and `TitleBarSlot`/`TitleBarControls` gating contributions on it; a future screen needing the same guarantee states it the same way. The outgoing side is unchanged, a scene being replaced staying `deactivating`, inert and pointer-silent because it is leaving. Result verified on the live route across three cold loads: maximum opacity while unreachable is **0.000**, and a real `page.mouse.click` during `entering` now dispatches inside the scene where it previously landed on an element behind it. Nothing changes visually — no control gains a loading state, no label flickers, and the entrance choreography is unchanged; only the moment the screen starts answering moves. Rejected on the way: dropping `inert` outright (hands an entering scene functional permission), and making every control paint a not-yet-live state until commit (a ~300ms label flicker at the end of every navigation, app-wide, that still loses the press) | accepted; refines [0421](0421-a-preparing-scene-has-no-permission-to-perform.md)/[0369](0369-one-cold-load-ladder-builds-background-then-chrome-then-scene.md)/[0307](0307-every-replaceable-region-is-a-director-owned-scene-slot.md) | 2026-08-11 |
| [0573](0573-a-game-is-read-back-without-being-taken-back.md) | A game is READ back without being taken back. The owner asked for "go back a move" and named the consideration himself — it is not an undo, so something has to track when you are not on the current move. That something is `reviewIndex: number | null` in the skirmish store, `null` meaning live, and it is the whole mechanism: two actions touch it and touch nothing else, so **no rule runs, no clock moves, no premove is dropped, and `game` is neither read nor written** — which is what makes reading the game back unable to cost the player the game they are still playing. This is the opposite act to the Run's paid Undo ([ADR-0556](0556-run-preparation-chooses-in-a-tab-column-of-one-line-rows.md) era), which RESTORES a board: costs gold, puts the clock back, drops the plies it took, and makes what it lands on live. The record it walks is new, because nothing in-tree held one: `undoStack` keeps a checkpoint per PLAYER move, only inside a Run, only while the economy can pay, and wipes entirely when a capture fails. `game/moveReview.ts` owns `PositionSnapshot` — the half of a `GameState` a move changes (`pieces`, `turn`, `winner`, `lastMove`, `halfmoveClock`, `positionCounts`) — because everything else is resolved at build and never moves again, so a board per half-move costs a pieces array rather than a duplicate terrain grid, which is what makes it affordable to hold for a long game AND to persist. `positions: RecordedPosition[]` pairs each snapshot with the half-move COUNT it stood at rather than inferring it from the array index, so a match saved before this existed can resume holding one board already deep into the game and say so instead of claiming to be move one. One board is recorded per NOTATED half-move at the three commit sites (player commit, enemy reply, netplay relay); `resolveEnemyReply` now returns a snapshot per notated move because a multi-move reply's intermediate boards exist only inside its loop and a review that could not step through them would jump the reply whole; a commit that notates nothing (an admin position change is not a move) records nothing. Asking for the newest recorded position resolves to `null` rather than a cursor pointing at the same board — being at the end of the score sheet IS being live, and a cursor that merely agreed would keep the battlefield read-only for no reason the player could see. Exactly two moments move the cursor by themselves: the commit that DECIDES the game (a result lands on the real board, and reviewing the finished game is free from there) and a paid Undo, which really did take moves back and so truncates the history to the restored log's ply count. Every other commit leaves it where it was — the score sheet grows underneath the player rather than yanking them forward. Rendering goes through the battlefield's EXISTING passive-position seam (`SkirmishBoard.surfaceState`, the one Deployment projects through) rather than a second path in: the seam gains `kind`, where a `plan` is a position being built and a `review` is an earlier board of the same live match — both read-only, differing in that a review keeps what no move can change (grid, authored promotion cells) and loses what is derived from where the pieces stand (legal moves, threat squares), which would be a lie about an older board. The review surface takes the SAME camera identity the live board computes for itself so stepping never reframes, and declares its units `settled` so a piece a step back brings to life appears at its seat instead of playing an entrance. Both CI gates that pinned `surfaceState={presentedDeploymentSurface}` now pin the ordered form, keeping a second projection path shut. ONE transport component in two homes (under the Event Log, and on a plate over the board while reviewing) so they cannot drift; arrows step and deliberately accept auto-repeat, Home/End take the ends, Escape drops to live and is claimed ONLY while a review is open; every logged move with a recorded board is pressable and the shown row is marked. `LOG_LIMIT` 24 to 600, because the log is now the navigation surface and a row that fell off the end is a move that can no longer be reached. The clock KEEPS RUNNING while you read — pausing it would make reviewing a free think — which is why the plate is unmissable rather than a quiet indicator. The plate is seated by a plain wrapper, not by its own rule: it wears the registered inner chrome frame whose `position: relative` beats a class on the plate later in the same stylesheet, the exact trap [ADR-0570](0570-a-board-anchored-callout-is-placed-in-screen-pixels-and-stays-inside-the-battlefield.md) documents — shipped that way first and the in-flow box took a grid row from `.skirmish-field` and halved the board it was describing. No RunSaveVersion, migration or gameplay rule change; `positions` is an optional persisted field, so an older save simply reviews nothing | accepted; refines [0570](0570-a-board-anchored-callout-is-placed-in-screen-pixels-and-stays-inside-the-battlefield.md) | 2026-08-11 |
| [0574](0574-a-stated-camera-boundary-governs-how-far-out-the-camera-goes.md) | **A stated camera boundary governs the zoom floor alone.** The two questions are **coverage** (`coverageTier` — the furthest-out rung at which the visible rectangle is still entirely INSIDE the level's boundary, rounding UP because a rung of slack outside it is exposed black) and **usefulness** (`zoomTierRange().outer`, unchanged — the first rung at which the whole level box fits inside the viewport). The zoom ladder (#865) was right to replace the per-window float [ADR-0301](0301-levels-own-an-authored-camera-coverage-boundary.md)'s floor produced with a rung on a global ladder, and that is kept; what it also did was replace the QUESTION, asking whether the boundary fits inside the VIEWPORT rather than whether the viewport fits inside the BOUNDARY. Those are opposites — a boundary that fits inside the viewport is by definition surrounded by whatever lies beyond it — so the widest view was GUARANTEED to show the boundary plus a margin of unpainted world, and ADR-0301 stayed `accepted` with its zoom-out clause intact while the code did the reverse. Neither limit subsumes the other: coverage alone cannot stop a camera retreating forever on a level whose backdrop is locked to the viewport, and usefulness alone cannot keep a camera out of world nobody painted. Two gaps compounded it. **The camera was measured on the wrong rectangle** — the Play board is deliberately full-bleed, painting past its 4:3 stage and cut only at the screen edge so it floats behind the title bar and HUD instead of being sliced under them, while the floor and the pan clamp were both derived from the stage; on a 2400x1100 window that stage is 1349px wide and the art paints across 2028px, leaving ~680px answerable to no boundary at all. Coverage is now enforced on the rectangle art is VISIBLE in, resolved from the nearest clipping ancestor in the live DOM so a CSS change to where the board is cut moves the coverage rectangle with it rather than silently desynchronising the two — a wider window costs zoom range instead of showing black, which is what an authored boundary MEANS. **And the runtime boundary defaulted to the snap preset**, which is an authoring convenience sized tightly around the playable surface and normally far SMALLER than what a level already paints, so intersecting accepted pre-drawn pixels with it threw away real coverage on every level whose author never opened the Camera page: the boundary is now the box when authored, box ∩ art when both, the ART when only art, and **none at all** when neither, that last case being the viewport-locked backdrop that paints wherever the camera goes and therefore has no unpainted world to keep anyone out of. The Camera page draws the boundary a player actually GETS — an unauthored pre-drawn level shows its accepted pixels, not the default — and re-frames when the boundary RESOLVES rather than once per visit, because landing on the page by URL framed it against the placeholder some twelve seconds before the artwork arrived and never looked again (invisible for as long as the only thing ever drawn there was a default small enough to fit the opening view anyway). Measured on the live route, Hold the Bridge at 2400x1100: **213,672 unpainted board-region pixels before, 8 after** (dark art, not stage), holding across 1280x800 / 2400x1100 / 3440x1440 and unchanged by real drags of 3000px to every extreme and the corner. Costs, stated: a wider window now shows the same world at a larger scale rather than more world, and a level whose art falls short of its authored box is zoomed IN slightly instead of showing black — which makes short art visible as a framing cost rather than as a hole. The pre-drawn inspectors pass a boundary and no level box, returning them to the coverage-only floor they had before #865; the Level Editor canvas states neither limit and stays unclamped per ADR-0301. No gameplay, RunSaveVersion, save shape or database migration change **ADDENDUM (same day, after the owner still could neither zoom out nor see the whole board):** two defects in this ADR's own first implementation. The visible region was the WINDOW — coverageViewportForStage walked to the nearest clipping ancestor and landed on .skirmish-screen, which includes the strips under the opaque title bar and Controls rail, so the camera zoomed IN to keep art under chrome nobody can see through and paid for it out of the pixels a player can see; the rail is on the right, which is exactly where the board was cut. The region is now the board's own allocation, [data-shell-viewport-primary], which the shell already marks — at 2560x900 that alone moves the floor from 1.80 to 1.55 and the board fits. And a pre-drawn plate does NOT own every environment pixel: both .skirmish-screen.is-predrawn-board::before { content: none } and shouldLoadSkirmishWorldBackground deleted the battlefield backdrop on that premise, but Hold the Bridge's raster is 1450x816 world px and the scene layer paints nothing beyond it (read from canvas alpha), so anything outside was bare stage and the camera had to be held inside the raster — which clears the opening composition by ~3% vertically, less than one 5% ladder rung, so there was no rung to take and the range was ZERO at almost every window size. With the backdrop restored the floor becomes what it should always have been, **zooming out ends with the whole painting visible**: four rungs of range at 1920x1080 where there were none. Coverage still governs PAN, so the board cannot be dragged off the screen. **FINAL FORM:** the camera limit had FOUR behaviours depending on which case a level fell into (stored box, stored box intersected with artwork, artwork alone, or no boundary at all with a usefulness limit instead), indistinguishable on screen — the owner spent a session unable to answer why he could not zoom out, because answering it meant reconstructing which branch his level took, and the rectangle the Level Editor drew was on every level he actually plays a number recomputed each load rather than a value anyone had set (measured: 4 of 37 official levels carried a stored cam, and all four were tool-written test battles with fractional world coordinates). Now: **every level stores one camera box, the view stops at it, and that is the whole rule.** effectiveBoardCameraCoverPolygon returns the box and consults nothing else; boardZoomFloor has one branch and containBox is deleted; the box is measured against the board allocation so the camera never buys coverage for the strips under the opaque title bar and Controls rail. Keeping a box inside its artwork belongs to AUTHORING as an action that moves the box, never a runtime check that silently overrides it — a drawn rectangle that does not mean what it says is the defect. Backfilled 33 official levels with defaultBoardCameraBounds(cols,rows), the rectangle the editor already drew, so nothing moved on screen; 4 already had one; 3 carry no board code at all (off-l-pinned, off-l-high-ground, off-l-ten-battle-run-test-battle-i). Verified after the write: 40 levels before and after, none lost or gained, both campaigns intact, and each level's measured zoom floor equals the cover tier of its own stored box. **FIT TO ARTWORK:** the fit is an ACTION in three places answering with one function (largestBoxInsideBoardCameraPolygon): a **Fit to artwork** control on the Camera page shown only where artwork exists; an automatic refit when AI artwork is ASSIGNED, overwriting whatever was there because the artwork edge moved; and a backfill onto the nine existing AI-artwork levels, each now carrying a box equal to its own painting (hold-bridge 1450x816, fortress-gate 1360x765, seven test battles). A rectangular accepted region returns unchanged; a legacy warped quad gets the largest axis-aligned rectangle by scanning pairs of horizontal cuts. Trap for the next tool: the plate's stored worldBounds are relative to the board origin from boardLabMetrics — a hand-rolled origin put every plate hundreds of world px off and reported six of nine levels as needing to grow past their own art. The fit is measured from PAINT, not the frame: a plate's declared worldBounds are its raster frame and a ragged edge does not fill it (measured from alpha: Hold the Bridge paints 99.4%, Fortress Gate 73.9%), so fitting to the frame left unpainted world inside the box — a visible sliver of backdrop between the ragged edge and the boundary line. largestPaintedPlateRect scans the decoded plate for the largest all-painted rectangle; Hold the Bridge 1450x816 to 1436x806, Fortress Gate 1360x765 to 1140x639, five of nine unchanged. Cropping the rasters instead was considered and declined (two levels carry meaningful waste, against four new accepted artwork versions in production) — but the analysis is reusable: a crop needs the occlusion mask cropped identically, frameWidth/frameHeight/worldBounds shifted, and the move-highlight profile repointed at the new backgroundVersionId since it returns nothing on a mismatch, while environmentGeometrySha256 survives (it excludes the raster) and footprints survive (per-cell percentages, not raster pixels). | accepted; supersedes #865's contain-only floor and refines [0301](0301-levels-own-an-authored-camera-coverage-boundary.md)/[0302](0302-camera-authoring-is-a-dedicated-level-editor-page.md) | 2026-08-11 |
| [0575](0575-a-settled-identity-is-re-read-for-as-long-as-the-app-is-open.md) | **A settled identity is re-read for as long as the application is open.** ADR-0306's owner answered "who is this" once and then stopped, returning early forever on `snapshot.status?.reachable`; after that the shared identity moved only when some account-gated call happened to report a 401. A session expires on a schedule the browser cannot see, so a tab open past that moment presented a signed-in account over a session that had ended — and playing needs no session (ADR-0060), so nothing contradicted the claim. That is how the hourly sign-out stayed invisible for two weeks, and why a backend restart looked causal: a restart forces a reload, and the reload was the only thing that ever re-asked. `watchAuthSession()` now binds to the application once at bootstrap. One throttled `wake(minGapMs)` serves both callers — the lifecycle passes a short floor because someone returning to the tab wants an answer now, the cadence tick passes `reprobeIntervalMs()` — and it resolves `null` when nothing was read rather than handing back a stale snapshot as a fresh answer. The cadence varies by phase and that asymmetry is the point: 20s while signed out, because someone is probably signing in right now in this or another tab; 5 minutes while signed in, because nothing is waiting on the answer. Hidden documents are skipped, and `wake` never races the startup probe's own retry loop. The Level Editor's identical scheduler is DELETED (ADR-0059) while its single self-healing read on entering `signed-out` stays; `App.tsx`'s scene-failure loop stays because it re-reads to retry a scene at 3s, not to refresh identity, and already calls the shared owner. Supersedes ADR-0519's probe and the reasoning that scoped re-reading to the paused state — the general case was what needed it. |
| [0576](0576-authentication-is-a-server-held-session.md) | **Authentication is a server-held session; the browser holds only an identifier.** Chess Tactics is a Backend-For-Frontend and was doing the inverse of what draft-ietf-oauth-browser-based-apps-26 §6.1.1 requires: the access and refresh tokens WERE the browser's cookies. One structural choice produced a family of defects — nothing to revoke, so sign-out expired two cookies and left the tokens valid upstream for up to seven days; no identity to cache, so every authenticated request made an HTTP call to `auth.romaine.life`; a session lifetime that was a cookie's `Max-Age` of 3600 seconds; and a pending sign-in in a process `Map` that a restart destroyed. Migration 77 adds `auth_sessions` and `auth_login_attempts`. Only the SHA-256 of a session token is stored, so a database read yields nothing replayable. Claims are cached on the row and re-read only on renewal, which takes the provider off the hot path. Three deadlines answer three questions: `idle_expires_at` slides to 30 days and is what a returning player is measured against, `absolute_expires_at` is fixed at sign-in at 90 days and no activity moves it, and `authenticated_at` is when credentials were last presented, which is what the 8-hour admin window reads. Renewal is how revocation propagates: a refresh refused 4xx ends the session, a 5xx is an outage and changes nothing. `SameSite=Strict` on the session cookie IS the named CSRF defence (§6.1.3.3.1); the 10-minute login-state cookie stays `Lax` deliberately, because the callback is a cross-site top-level navigation and `Strict` would fail every sign-in. The client is confidential and asks for `offline_access` — the scope without which the provider returns no refresh token at all. Re-authentication re-arms the session in hand rather than minting a new one, or the absolute deadline would reset every 8 hours and never fire. Every existing session is invalidated on deploy. |
| [0577](0577-a-session-grant-is-never-triggered-by-a-request-header.md) | **A session grant is never triggered by a request header, and the CSRF defence is named rather than emergent.** The dev bypass had two triggers and only one was gated on `DEV_AUTH`: any Host containing `.tank.dev.romaine.life` — a SUBSTRING match — granted a session for a fixed cookie value with no credential at all. Production never received such a request because the Gateway pins `hostnames: [chess-tactics.com]` and `trust proxy` is off, which means the only thing standing between an unauthenticated session grant in the production binary and the internet was a routing rule in a different layer. The same chart already knew better: `PUBLIC_ORIGIN` is pinned from `.Values.hostname` precisely so the redirect URI never reflects a client-controllable header. The host trigger is DELETED, not flagged off (`docs/migration-policy.md`); deployed slots sign in through the real provider like every other lane. Separately, cross-site writes were already blocked — by `SameSite=Lax` plus no CORS header anywhere — which was adequate and entirely accidental: nothing stated it and nothing tested it, so a CORS header or a cookie change would have removed it silently. `backend/authCookiePolicy.test.js` now fails if the session cookie stops being `Strict`, if anything but the login-state cookie is `Lax`, if a CORS header appears, if the retired token cookies reappear, or if the host trigger returns. Admin writes additionally require an authentication under 8 hours old, answered as RFC 9470's `insufficient_user_authentication` so the browser can tell a step-up challenge from a sign-out — treating one as the other would sign the whole shell out over a live session. |
| [0578](0578-supersampled-render-downscale-is-native-generation.md) | Rendering a source model at a multiple of delivery size and collapsing it is NATIVE generation, not the spatial resampling ADR-0076 blocks. The two are opposite in provenance: a supersampled render reconstructs the model at every sample and averages what the pixel actually covers — the same thing Blender's own pixel filter does, at a ratio where it does more good — while a recapture resizes finished delivery art, destroying decisions already made and closing a circular provenance loop. `unitAssetProductionEligibility` now reads structured provenance rather than the fact of resampling: `spatialResampling` alone no longer condemns a candidate, `pipeline: 'supersampled-render'` is admitted, and `accepted-sprite-recapture` and the stored `acceptanceBlockReason` still mean never. This is what lets a deterministic Blender filter deliver the roster: the filter needs block-sized room to work in, so it renders at `sprite × block` and collapses the blocks to art pixels, which is a downscale by construction | accepted; refines ADR-0076 | 2026-08-11 |
| [0579](0579-card-art-is-briefed-from-a-king-rooted-event.md) | **Card art is briefed from a king-rooted event, not from a background anchor.** Two cards shared an illustration because ADR-0520 keys art to `(footprint, roster)`, and under `cardSpan: 2` the 69 dealable cards draw on 36 images, 13 of them shared and three shared six ways. The deeper fault was measurable: 94 briefs carried 36 distinct scenes, 46 contained the word "covered", 30 were beside water, 21 mentioned flowers, and every one ended by removing the significant thing from frame — because card art had been briefed from `docs/lore/historical-anchors/`, whose README scopes those anchors to BACKGROUND sets and states "the tragedy happened, but the image does not show it directly". A background contract applied to a surface it was never written for. Cards now have their own contract (`docs/art/card-art-brief-contract.md`): residue is a subject and absence is not; every brief names one act and holds one pregnant instant of it; the fifteen Kings are cast first because no king can feel cheap, each anchored to one documented act, with the theatre count unbounded and following card quality rather than one war; every formation rolls up to exactly one King's world. Art becomes one illustration per card — 69 plus 15. Price contributes nothing and the `arrangement` field is deleted, both at the owner's direction; hieratic scale ("much the largest in frame", present in 14 of 15 King briefs) goes with it while the monarch's eye exception stays. The act, the Cessolis cast reading and the printed register are three layers, not three options, split on the existing rule that the anchor is "for us, not for the viewer to be able to identify from a caption" | accepted; supersedes ADR-0520's art keying and `arrangement`, refines ADR-0282 and the historical-anchor README | 2026-08-11 |
| [0580](0580-both-forces-material-shares-one-box.md) | **Both forces' material shares ONE box, seated ahead of the battle clock, on every play surface.** A player could not see what either army was worth: the HUD's pips count UNITS, and two units can be a Queen and a King (9) or two Pawns (2). The game already prices a unit in one place — `PIECE_VALUE`, Pawn 1 · Knight 3 · Bishop 3 · Rook 5 · Queen 9 · King 0, read through `manubiaeUnitWorth` — and Deditio pays two gold per point of enemy force standing at the mate, so the enemy's material was already a live gold forecast that was computed, paid on, and never shown. This shipped FIRST as a matched pair of boxes flanking the clock, to protect a documented property of that row (the two flank chips share one width, described as putting the clock dead centre over the title bar's nailhead diamond). Nelson rejected it on sight and was right: splitting a comparison and putting the clock between its halves makes the reader do the readout's job. Rebalancing the flanks around a single box would have cost the objective **144px of empty box**, so the alignment being protected was checked on the running app first — `.app-shell-titlebar-stud` and the retry stud sharing its seat both draw NOTHING on a Battle route (captured at 4x: bare bar). The clock was being lined up with an ornament the player cannot see. So: one box, one frame, one hover target. Inside it each force is a **live Pawn sprite in that army's own palette** then its points — with both numbers in one frame the marks are the ONLY thing saying which is which, so the distinction is carried in real game art; and a Pawn is the 1 the scale is denominated in. The gap BETWEEN the forces is wider than the gap inside each, which is the whole structure the box needs and why it has no internal rail. The tip spells the scale out FROM `PIECE_VALUE` so the words cannot drift from the arithmetic. The reading is a pure function (`battleMaterialReadout`) returning both forces with the reader's own first via `clientSideOrder`, so a lobby guest on `enemy` sees their own force in the first seat. One reader prices both sides — `standingForceValue(pieces, side)`, with `standingEnemyForceValue` now that function with the enemy filled in — so what a player watches during the Battle cannot disagree with what the mate pays. The box is seated exactly where the clock is and rides in and out with it: points exist only while there is a board to count them on, so in the Run's bar it is not among the durable measures. The row KEEPS what is visible (cluster page-centred; turn plate and objective still one shared width, a mirrored pair) and gives up what was not (the clock is no longer the row's midpoint — 72px right of the stud at 1440). Narrow widths shed material, never something that was there first: ≤1180 tightens it with the Run's measures and drops the stable two-digit width; ≤860 drops the box, which is marginal rather than obvious and was measured — at 740 the row without it is 332px against a 440px budget and the tightened box is ~90px, fitting by ~14px, the entire margin, gone the moment both forces read two digits. | accepted | 2026-08-11 |
| [0581](0581-a-downscale-of-a-generated-source-is-native.md) | **A downscale of a generated source is native, and needs no exception.** Regenerating all 84 card illustrations through `codex-image-gen` — which renders far above the 400x280 card window and comes down to it — would have made every one of them an owner-approved production exception, and the ADR-0520 door it would have used is cut to the FAMILY slot grammar (`^ui/run/card-art/[0-9]+-[pkbrq]+/…$`), which refuses `f-011011-bpr`, `b` and `k-sole-surviving-issue` alike; the batch could not have been accepted at all. Six such per-decision branches already existed (ADR-0332/0360/0414/0506/0520/0560), each bought because no general form existed. `supersampled-native-v1` is that general form: a closed `sourceKind` of `generation` or `render`, `native1x: true`, a strictly larger named source, a named transform, and SHAs binding both the source raster and these exact bytes. Recapture — resizing already-accepted delivery art, which is what ADR-0075/0076 was actually defending against — stays forbidden and stays UNSAYABLE, because there is no `accepted-asset` value to write in a closed set. The six existing branches are untouched, since accepted rows cannot be patched; they are legacy doors nothing new needs, and a seventh should never be written. No migration: `native_evidence` is a JSON column | accepted; extends [0578](0578-supersampled-render-downscale-is-native-generation.md), refines [0076](0076-scaling-is-calibration-production-art-is-native-1x.md) | 2026-08-12 |
| [0582](0582-run-preparation-tabs-are-named-for-the-act.md) | **Run preparation's two rail tabs are named for the ACT — Continue and New — not for the object behind them.** ADR-0556 had already cut those rows to their name and nothing else, on the rule that a row whose whole job is to open a detail column does not summarize it; that makes the name the entire control, and **Current Run** was not naming a control. It named the object and left the press to be inferred — an object the surface states three more times anyway, in the detail column's first facts and in the replacement note. **Start New Run** failed the other way: a verb phrase beside a noun phrase, so the pair did not read as one choice, and its "Start" duplicates the **Start Run** verb the column it opens closes with. **Start** is not the tab's to carry — what a new Run costs the one being held is ADR-0557's replacement note and the armed **Abandon and Start**, both in the column the tab opens. ADR-0334's **No active Run** detail line is untouched. The detail landmarks are **Continue** and **New Run**, the second keeping its subject because a landmark is announced away from the rail that supplied it. Addresses, test ids and media slots keep `current` / `new`: they are identities, not labels, and renaming an installed slot is a production content change with nothing to gain. Supersedes ADR-0289's naming clause only. | accepted | 2026-08-11 |
| [0583](0583-a-camera-box-is-centred-on-the-board-and-sized-to-a-display.md) | **A camera box is centred on the board and sized to a display — two actions, each changing exactly one of position and size.** Every existing write to the boundary decided both at once: Snap from the board, Fit from the painting, Set from view from the canvas, a drag from whichever handle was grabbed. So the slack between the playable grid and the box — which IS the distance a player can scroll in that direction — could only be evened out by throwing away the size that had been composed against the artwork by eye. **Centre on grid** moves the box and keeps its width and height; the page now states the four scroll distances (`Room left / right`, `Room above / below`) and disables the action while they already match, because that is a statement about the box as much as a button.
